Market Research Report

Startup Name Study

Synthetic market research for a WhatsApp-first practical life-help service for AI-distant and AI-unaware adults. The report includes the 72 respondent run, aggregate score evidence, the final DeepSeek Pro synthesis, and the final Qwen Max founder memo.

Answer

Use Melbury

Melbury is the undisputed winner for your WhatsApp-first life-help service, capturing 68% of first-choice votes and dominating across all core scenarios and demographics. It uniquely passes the 'Grandparent Test' by sounding like a trusted, local, physical institution rather than a tech startup or scam. Brinsley is the only viable backup but suffers from 'expensive/formal' perceptions. All other names (Azrel, Serel, Fontmell, Hawsker, Azriela) trigger fatal scam, pharmaceutical, or tech-abstraction fears and must be immediately retired. The primary risk is Melbury's slight 'old-fashioned' feel and potential geographic confusion, which can be mitigated with warm onboarding messaging and clear trademark/domain checks.

Primary

Melbury is the strongest name for broad trust, WhatsApp legitimacy, and low scam risk.

Backup

Brinsley is viable if Melbury is unavailable, but it needs softer, less expensive-feeling positioning.

Drop

Azrel, Serel, Fontmell, Hawsker, Azriela carry too much scam, tech, spelling, or category confusion risk for the main brand.

Judge 1

DeepSeek Pro Full Synthesis

Melbury is the clear winner across all quantitative and qualitative dimensions, dominating both aggregate scores (overall 4.45) and final choice counts (49 of 72 winners). It consistently signals 'local, established, human-scale help' rather than 'tech product' or 'scam', which is the critical trust threshold for this AI‑distant audience. Brinsley is the only viable runner‑up but falls significantly behind on approachability, scam risk, and practical‑help associations. Every other name (Hawsker, Serel, Azriela, Fontmell, Azrel) triggers fatal scam, drug, or tech abstractions and must be rejected.

Recommendation

Melbury primary, Brinsley backup.

Adopt 'Melbury' as the service name for the WhatsApp‑first practical life‑help product. Its performance is far superior to all alternatives, and the qualitative evidence shows it uniquely bridges the trust gap for AI‑distant adults. Reserve 'Brinsley' only as a backup if Melbury cannot be secured (trademark, domain, or platform restrictions). Do not use Hawsker, Serel, Azriela, Fontmell, or Azrel. Prioritize real‑world validation with the most vulnerable segments (older adults, scam‑sensitive, immigrant families) to confirm the synthetic findings before final legal lock‑in.

Confidence

high

72 synthetic respondents generated highly consistent scores, qualitative patterns, and segment‑level corroboration; the margin between Melbury and all alternatives is decisive, and the primary drivers (scam fear, local‑place heuristic) align with known real‑world behaviour of the target demographic.

Evidence Quality

Synthetic data from a 72‑respondent Qwen 35B full rich run, with 340k tokens indicating depth. Patterns are internally robust—clear score cliffs between tiers, strong quote alignment, and consistent segment‑wise agreement. Limitation: no real‑human validation, possible UK‑centric name biases, no live WhatsApp environment, and no trademark/availability checks.

Key Findings

  • Melbury dominates overall scores (4.45) and final choice counts (49 winners, 68% of respondents), with a 71‑point lead over Brinsley in rank‑points (472 vs. 401).
  • Scam risk is the decisive factor: names with 'Az'‑prefix (Azrel, Azriela) or tech‑like sounds (Fontmell, Serel) show extremely high blind‑scam‑risk scores (Azrel 4.49, Serel 3.71) and trigger immediate block‑or‑delete reactions from respondents.
  • Brinsley is the only alternative with any traction (11 winners, 33 runner‑up picks) but suffers from perceptions of being 'too fancy', 'expensive', and less approachable, limiting its fit for anxious, modest‑income users.
  • Across all six service scenarios (healthcare, household, letters, scam texts, school notes, WhatsApp legitimacy) Melbury leads by 2‑5x over the next name, confirming that the name fits the real‑world triggers where users would reach out.
  • The 'local place' heuristic (real‑world geography, care home, council office) is the strongest trust cue; Melbury’s AI‑distance score of 4.94 reflects that it entirely avoids digital‑abstraction fears.
  • Human‑name appeal (Azriela) works only for narrow immigrant and young‑adult‑family segments but is heavily rejected elsewhere as 'foreign scam' or 'too long', making it unusable as the primary brand.
  • Spelling and pronunciation simplicity matters: Melbury (4.83) far outpaces Azriela (1.96) and Fontmell (2.49), reducing cognitive friction for users who are already anxious.
  • Practical‑helper wins in some sub‑segments (rural, digitally anxious worker) for Hawsker (6 winners) but the name's overall scam‑association (blind‑scam‑risk 3.32) makes it a high‑risk option for the broader audience.

Name-By-Name Assessment

Melbury
Verdict
Strongly recommended – use as the primary name
Strengths
  • Sounds like a real, visit‑able place (care home, council service, local office)
  • Zero tech jargon; highest AI‑distance score (4.94)
  • Very low scam risk (low_scam_risk_score 4.58) and highest blind trust (3.83)
  • Easy to spell and pronounce (4.83); respondents say 'easy to spell' repeatedly
  • High scenario‑fit consistency: 53/72 for healthcare, 52/72 for letters/forms, 48/72 for WhatsApp legitimacy
Risks
  • Perceived as 'slightly old‑fashioned' – could feel dusty for younger users, but that anchors trust for the core demographic
  • Risk of confusion with a specific care home chain or a real town (e.g., Melbury Osmond, Dorset) – check trademark and geography
  • Slightly long to type for some (6 letters), though not a dealbreaker given memorability
Evidence
Aggregate overall 4.45; 49 winners; dominant in all segment‑winner counts except a handful of dissenting segments; qualitative quotes: 'Melbury is the only name that sounds like a legitimate, local business entity rather than a tech startup or a scam', 'It sounds like a place I can walk into if things go wrong'.
Brinsley
Verdict
Acceptable runner‑up – use only if Melbury is unavailable
Strengths
  • Sounds established and professional ('like a solicitor or estate agent')
  • Pleasant to say and pronounce; easy to spell (3.83)
  • Not techy – acceptable AI distance (4.35)
  • Strong second in final counts (11 winners, 33 runner‑up, rank‑points 401)
Risks
  • Triggers 'expensive' and 'too fancy for my budget' concerns, creating a barrier for low‑income or benefits‑client users
  • Feels like a store or a formal firm, not a practical helper; approachability score only 3.10
  • Lower help‑fit (3.10) and higher scam risk (3.54) than Melbury; some users fear it’s a 'posh lawyer' who will charge
  • Length (8 letters) and perceived formality may reduce quick‑text willingness
Evidence
Aggregate overall 3.54; 11 winners; selected quotes: 'Brinsley sounds like a real person in the village who knows how to handle paperwork. I’d trust them with my bills', but also 'feels more expert' and 'might charge'.
Hawsker
Verdict
Reject – scam associations outweigh any practical appeal
Strengths
  • Short (7 letters) and distinctive; some users read it as 'practical' and 'local service'
  • Gained 6 winner picks, mostly in digitally anxious worker and rural practical helper segments
Risks
  • Sounds like 'hacker', 'hawk' (predator), 'hawker' (street seller) – blind scam risk 3.32, trust only 2.35
  • Phonetic proximity to cyber‑threat terms makes it immediately blockable for scam‑sensitive older adults
  • No human warmth; industry association is typically wrong (e.g., 'sounds like a fishing tackle shop', not help)
Evidence
Aggregate overall 2.62; blind‑scam‑risk 3.32; quote: 'Hawsker sounds like a trick to steal my money' (r001); even winning respondents wanted it for 'fixing a leak', not for sensitive paperwork, limiting its fit for core scenarios like healthcare or forms.
Serel
Verdict
Reject – vague and strongly associated with pharmaceuticals/scams
Strengths
  • Short (5 letters) and easy to say; soft sound initially appeals
  • Some users found it 'clean' and 'easy to pronounce'
Risks
  • Sounds like a drug name or drug side effect – 'Sounds like a drug', 'Sounds like a drug name' are top concerns
  • Very high blind‑scam risk (3.71); vague meaning creates zero trust
  • Lowest AI distance among rejects (2.28) – feels abstract and tech‑adjacent
  • Zero winner picks; 6 reject picks; lowest WhatsApp legitimacy score (2.00)
Evidence
Aggregate overall 2.32; quote: 'Serel sounds like a virus I'd block immediately' (r012); scenario fit counts near zero for forms (1) and household (1).
Azriela
Verdict
Reject – polarising human‑name effect not robust enough for broad use
Strengths
  • Sounds like a human being to some immigrant and family‑translator segments (4 winners there)
  • Distinctive and soft; easy to say if already familiar with the name
Risks
  • Too long (7 letters) and hard to spell (1.96) – top concern 'too long to type'
  • Feels foreign and distant to many; triggers 'religious scam from overseas' associations
  • Low AI distance (2.67) and very low WhatsApp legitimacy (1.90)
  • Rejected heavily by older adult scam‑sensitive and caregiver segments (e.g., 'Azriela sounds like a scam trying to steal my mom’s info')
Evidence
Aggregate overall 2.28; blind‑scam‑risk 3.51; 4 winners, 6 reject picks; quotes: 'Azriela sounds like a real person I can trust' (r019) vs. 'Azriela sounds like a scam charity I'd just delete' (r018). The human‑name appeal is real but fragile.
Fontmell
Verdict
Reject – niche 'form‑help' association cannot overcome tech/coldness
Strengths
  • Unique and sounds established; some users read 'Font' as signalling letters/forms
  • 2 winner picks (tech‑avoidant household manager, older adult scam‑sensitive) where 'Fontmell sounds like a local business that actually helps with paper'
Risks
  • Hard to spell (2.49) and hard to remember; sounds technical
  • No human warmth; approachability 1.81, lowest among all names
  • 'Sounds like a bill collector' or 'tech support scam' – blind‑scam‑risk 3.53
  • Negative associations: 'Mell' triggers death/medical fear in some respondents
Evidence
Aggregate overall 2.22; 2 winners, 16 reject picks; quote: 'Fontmell sounds like a local business that actually helps with paper' (r004) but also 'I wouldn't trust Azrel or Fontmell with my kid's info—they sound like hackers' (r016).
Azrel
Verdict
Strongly reject – highest scam risk, dangerous associations
Strengths
  • Short; unique spelling; superficially 'modern'
Risks
  • Highest blind‑scam‑risk (4.49); lowest overall score (1.50)
  • Sounds like a virus, Angel of Death (Azrael), a drug name, or a crypto scam
  • No human warmth; abstract and cold; extremely low trust (1.42)
  • 38 reject picks; no winner picks; worst scenario fit across all categories
Evidence
Aggregate overall 1.50; quotes: 'I wouldn't trust Azrel… sounds like a virus', 'Azrael (Angel of Death)' (r003); the name is an immediate hard‑fail for every respondent segment except perhaps a few abstract‑tolerant outliers.

Segment Caveats

  • Immigrant household admin and young adult family translator segments showed a preference for Azriela (4 and 2 winners respectively) because it sounds like a human name, bypassing the 'bot' filter. In these segments, a personal‑name brand could outperform a place‑based brand.
  • Digitally anxious worker and rural practical helper segments each gave Hawsker 3 and 1 winners respectively, drawn to its 'local fixer' vibe. This suggests that a blunt, practical‑sounding name has niche appeal, but Hawsker’s scam undertone still makes it too risky.
  • The tech‑avoidant household manager segment included 1 Fontmell winner who explicitly read 'Font' as a cue for forms/paperwork. If targeting a very narrow 'form‑phobic' sub‑audience, Fontmell’s semantic hint could be explored, but the coldness and spelling issues remain.
  • Overall, these dissents are small, and Melbury still places second or third in every dissenting segment, often winning when the segment contains older adults. The headline recommendation of Melbury holds for broadest reach; Azriela could be considered only if the service plans to micro‑target immigrant communities with a different brand front.

Risks And Limitations

  • Synthetic data limitation: All evidence comes from a single Qwen 35B run; real older, AI‑distant adults may have different phonetic associations, cultural references, and scam triggers that the model did not capture.
  • UK‑centric bias: Melbury and Brinsley are real UK place names; their trustworthiness may not translate to non‑UK audiences. The study did not test US, Asian, or other cultural contexts.
  • No trademark/domain/WhatsApp Business name availability check: Melbury could be taken or restricted, forcing a compromise.
  • Potential confusion: Melbury is a small village in Dorset; users might think the service is geographically tied to that location, causing credibility issues if not handled transparently.
  • Over‑reliance on scam‑fear: The synthetic respondents may exaggerate scam‑sensitivity compared to real users; live testing could find that some of the rejected names are not as instantly toxic.
  • The 'blind' scores (presented without service context) may not reflect the effect of seeing the name alongside a helpful first message in a real WhatsApp chat; trust might be built faster once a message is read.

Follow-Up Real User Tests

  • Conduct moderated WhatsApp simulation tests with 15‑20 real older adults (65+) and scam‑sensitive caregivers, showing a first message from 'Melbury' vs. 'Brinsley' and measuring trust, open rates, and willingness to share a photo of a bill.
  • Run A/B tests in community settings (library, care home, community centre) using printed WhatsApp screenshots to gauge immediate 'would you trust this?' reactions for Melbury and Brinsley.
  • Test Azriela with 10‑15 real immigrant household administrators and young adult family translators to validate the human‑name advantage and identify any cultural pitfalls.
  • Check trademark databases (UK and target markets) and WhatsApp Business display name policies for Melbury; confirm domain and social handle availability.
  • Investigate real‑world phonetic associations: run a quick street‑style audio test where respondents hear the name aloud and associate it, to ensure 'Melbury' does not trigger unintended negative schemas.
Final Pass

Qwen Max Founder Memo

Melbury is the undisputed winner for your WhatsApp-first life-help service, capturing 68% of first-choice votes and dominating across all core scenarios and demographics. It uniquely passes the 'Grandparent Test' by sounding like a trusted, local, physical institution rather than a tech startup or scam. Brinsley is the only viable backup but suffers from 'expensive/formal' perceptions. All other names (Azrel, Serel, Fontmell, Hawsker, Azriela) trigger fatal scam, pharmaceutical, or tech-abstraction fears and must be immediately retired. The primary risk is Melbury's slight 'old-fashioned' feel and potential geographic confusion, which can be mitigated with warm onboarding messaging and clear trademark/domain checks.

Decision

Adopt 'Melbury' as the primary brand name immediately. Reserve 'Brinsley' strictly as a backup in case Melbury is unavailable due to trademark, domain, or WhatsApp Business policy restrictions. Drop all other names.

Final Guidance

Lock in 'Melbury' as your primary brand immediately, but do not sign final legal or marketing contracts until you complete a 48-hour trademark, domain, and WhatsApp Business display name availability check. If Melbury is blocked, pivot to 'Brinsley' and aggressively counter its 'expensive' perception with 'free, friendly help' messaging. Discard all other names. Your immediate next step is to build a high-fidelity WhatsApp prototype using the Melbury name and run the 15-person real-user simulation test to confirm the synthetic data's trust margins before scaling.

Why Melbury Works

  • Passes the 'Local Place' Heuristic: Sounds like a real, visit-able entity (care home, council office, local business), which is the ultimate trust signal for AI-distant adults.
  • Zero Tech/Scam Triggers: Highest AI-distance score (4.94) and lowest blind scam risk (1.32). It completely bypasses the bot/phishing filters in users' minds.
  • High Scenario Fit: Dominates across all critical use cases, including healthcare appointments (53/72), letters/forms (52/72), and scam text verification (44/72).
  • Frictionless Typing: High spelling and pronunciation score (4.83) ensures users can easily save the contact and search for it without anxiety.

Why Brinsley Loses

  • Perceived as Expensive: Frequently triggers 'too fancy for my budget' and 'feels like a store/lawyer' concerns, creating a barrier for low-income or benefits-reliant users.
  • Lower Approachability: Scores significantly lower on approachability (3.10 vs Melbury's 4.12) and help-fit, feeling more like a formal firm than a practical, warm helper.
  • Typing Friction: At 8 letters, it is slightly long for quick WhatsApp typing, and its formal tone lacks the immediate community warmth needed for vulnerable users.

Names To Drop

  • Azrel reason: Fatal risk. Sounds like a virus, crypto scam, or the Angel of Death. Immediate block/delete.
  • Serel reason: Fatal risk. Sounds like a pharmaceutical drug or medical side effect. Zero trust or help association.
  • Fontmell reason: High risk. Sounds like a bill collector or tech support scam. Cold, hard to spell, and triggers death/medical fears.
  • Hawsker reason: High risk. Phonetic link to 'hacker' or 'hawker' triggers severe scam suspicion, despite niche appeal as a 'fixer'.
  • Azriela reason: High risk. Polarizing human name. While it appeals to some immigrant segments, it is heavily rejected as a 'foreign religious scam' and is too long/hard to spell.

Segment Caveats

  • Immigrant & Young Adult Translators: Showed minority preference for 'Azriela' (human name bypasses bot fear). Mitigation: Use human-sounding agent personas (e.g., 'Ask Amina') within the Melbury brand for these communities.
  • Digitally Anxious Workers & Rural Helpers: Tied or showed minority preference for 'Hawsker' due to its 'practical fixer' vibe. Mitigation: Melbury may feel too bureaucratic for urgent physical fixes; use warm, action-oriented onboarding messaging.
  • Older Scam-Sensitive & Tech-Avoidant: Small minority preferred 'Brinsley' (expert cue) or 'Fontmell' (form-help cue). Mitigation: Explicitly state 'free help' and 'paperwork assistance' in Melbury's WhatsApp bio to cover these semantic gaps.

WhatsApp Launch Implications

  • Display Name Trust: On WhatsApp, the display name is the only trust signal before a message is opened. Melbury's 'local place' feel ensures it won't be preemptively blocked like tech-sounding names.
  • Contact Saving: Melbury (7 letters) is easy to spell and save. Avoid adding corporate suffixes (e.g., 'Melbury AI' or 'Melbury Tech') which will destroy the organic trust heuristic.
  • Expectation Management: Because Melbury sounds like a place/institution, users won't expect a 1-to-1 human friend (unlike Azriela), reducing frustration when they interact with an AI or structured service.
  • First Message Strategy: The initial auto-reply must immediately bridge the gap between 'local place' and 'practical help' (e.g., 'Hi, this is Melbury. Send us a photo of your letter, bill, or warning light, and we'll help you sort it.').

72 Respondents

Respondent Detail

r007 · adult_child_caregiver Winner: Melbury Runner-up: Brinsley Reject: Azrel

43-year-old in the US Midwest; lives alone. They are a adult child coordinating care and paperwork for an older parent. Digital posture: uses a smartphone constantly but has little patience for new apps or vague AI promises. Research-relevant need: fast help with care appointments, pharmacy notes, insurance letters, and confusing instructions.

Overall Ranking

Melbury > Brinsley > Fontmell > Azriela > Serel > Hawsker > Azrel

Decision Reasoning

Melbury is the only name that feels like a legitimate, established local entity without sounding like a tech scam or a medical drug. For a caregiver dealing with high-stress paperwork, 'trust' is the primary currency. Melbury sounds like a place you can walk into. Brinsley is a close second but feels too exclusive/expensive. Azrel and Hawsker trigger immediate scam defenses and must be rejected.

Verbatim Summary

Melbury is the only one that sounds like a real place my mom would trust. I don’t want another robot or a scam; I just want help with these forms, and Melbury feels like a neighbor who knows how to fix things.

Diagnostic Concern

Names starting with 'Az' or ending in 'ker' are immediately categorized as scams or bots by this persona. The 'adult child' segment is hyper-vigilant against fraud and will ignore anything that sounds like a tech startup or a foreign charity.
